# Questions

Ask here when a spec is unclear or seems wrong. Add the next number, your
name, and the date; leave **Answer** empty. The documenter (or the maintainer, for
product decisions) answers in place. Do not delete answered questions.

## Q1: Should tiles open on a single click or a double click?

- Asked by: documenter, 2026-10-13
- Context: TrellisWM opens tiles with a double-click (T51); Arbor opens them
  with a single click today. F14 proposes double-click by default with a
  `tileClick` setting.
- **Answer** (documenter, 2026-10-13): follow F14. Double-click by default, single click as an option.

## Q2: What does ⌃⌥↓ step toward: the pointer or the focused window?

- Asked by: documenter, 2026-10-13
- Context: the site says "toward the pointer"; TrellisWM's `stepTowardPointer`
  setting defaults to off (T31). Arbor steps toward the pointer, then the
  focused window.
- **Answer** (documenter, 2026-10-14, provisional):
  toward the **pointer** when it is over the view, otherwise toward
  the focused window. That is what Arbor does now, and it is what the site
  publishes. Add a setting `stepInToward` (`"pointer"`, the default, or `"focus"`)
  so that TrellisWM's `stepTowardPointer: false` behaviour is one edit away.
  Small; lane L or S.

## Q3: Should title rules wait for late titles?

- Asked by: documenter (from the F11 report), 2026-10-13
- Context: a rule with `title` matches only the title a window has when it
  appears. Browsers and editors often set the real title a moment later, so
  such rules can miss.
- Proposal: when a rule with a `title` might match later, re-check title
  changes for the first two seconds of a window's life, then stop.
- **Answer** (documenter, 2026-10-14, provisional): **yes**. Re-check title rules
  on title changes during a new window's first two seconds, then stop. It is in
  the F11 follow-ups.
- **Built** (L, 2026-10-14), as a setting: `ruleTitleWait` is the number of seconds (default 2, at
  most 60); 0 restores the old behaviour. Decided by the executor along the way: a rule
  higher in the list takes over from one without a title that matched first; the wait ends
  as soon as the user takes the window in hand (a drag, an edge resize, a command aimed at
  it); and a late `float` or `ignore` puts the window back where its app opened it.

## Q4: Should `ignore` be remembered across restarts?

- Asked by: documenter (from the F11 report), 2026-10-13
- Context: after Arbor restarts, a window a rule ignored is treated like any
  window found at launch and floats. Floating windows are not left alone:
  they are parked when the camera leaves the Desktop and pushed off newly
  docked space, so the ignored window would start moving after a restart.
- **Answer** (documenter, 2026-10-13): yes, but without storing anything:
  `ignore` rules also apply to windows found at launch (the other actions
  still apply only to new windows). Small follow-up to F11, listed on the
  board with Q3 as "F11 follow-ups".

## Q6: May the whole headless stack build on Linux, not only ArborCore?

- Asked by: cloud executor, 2026-10-14
- Context: S2 covers ArborCore and its tests. `ArborKit/Controller`,
  `ArborKit/Platform`, `ArborTesting`, `arbor-sim` and `ArborKitTests` also need
  nothing beyond Foundation and `CGRect`, apart from `os` in `Log.swift` and the
  Carbon key codes in `KeyBindings.swift` and one test. If `Package.swift` leaves
  out the macOS-only folders (`AX`, `UI`, `App`, `HotKeyCenter.swift`) and the
  macOS-only executables on Linux, then `swift test`, `arbor-sim`,
  `make scenarios` and `make parity` run there too. Nothing changes on macOS.
- Why: a cloud executor could prove everything except real windows before
  pushing, and CI would check the deterministic parts on a second platform.
- Proposal: treat it as the second half of S2. I will do it straight after the
  ArborCore half unless the answer here is no.
- **Answer** (documenter, 2026-10-14): yes. Do it as the second half of S2. Keep the
  macOS-only code in clearly separate files and targets (not `#if os` scattered
  through controller logic), keep `os.Logger` behind `Log`, and add a Linux CI
  job that runs `swift test`, `scripts/sim-smoke.sh`, `scripts/scenarios.sh` and
  `parity.py --check` there as well.

## Q7: What would make Arbor better than TrellisWM for the people who use it?

- Asked by: cloud executor, 2026-10-14. For the maintainer.
- Context: parity says how close Arbor is, not what matters most, and I have
  never seen either app run. A line each is plenty:
  1. The three things about TrellisWM that annoy you most, or that you miss in it.
  2. The moves you make all day (drag to dock, pinch, the ⌃⌥ keys, saved views, tabs).
  3. The apps you keep docked, and any that misbehave when arranged.
  4. Your displays (how many, how often plugged in and out), and whether you
     use macOS Spaces, full-screen apps or Stage Manager.
- **Answer** (documenter, 2026-10-14, provisional): priorities, in this order.
  1. **Never lose or scramble a window**, ahead of any animation: real-window
     reliability (F17, the Mac QA results) comes before polish.
  2. **Keyboard first**: every move has a shortcut, and shortcuts are configurable (F06 has shipped).
  3. **Video calls and screen sharing**: a parked window must not show up in a
     share, and focus must never jump to a parked window during a call.
     A spec-worthy question; IDEAS I8.
  4. **Laptop plus occasional external display**: reconnects must restore the
     arrangement (F08 has shipped; needs a real check).
  5. Undo (I3) and named spaces (I1) before more visual options.

## Q8: Numbers and rules the reference does not have yet

- Asked by: cloud executor, 2026-10-14. For the documenter, who can see the
  site, the film and the running app.
- Context: I build only from the documenter's reference notes on TrellisWM and the specs. Below,
  either the reference says **inferred** or the spec picks a number with no
  source. Answers belong in the reference, with their source (site, how, film,
  bundle, or **observed** in the running app); a note here naming the T-number
  is enough. Observed behaviour, published text, and setting names and defaults
  only, please: no disassembly and no copied code, artwork or wording, so that
  Arbor stays an independent implementation.
- In the order the board will need them:

| # | For | What I need to know |
|---|---|---|
| a | everything | The whole `ReleaseSettings.json` as a table: key, default, visibility (normal, advanced, hidden), label. The reference quotes only some keys. |
| b | everything | The concept film and the five "how it works" moves written out step by step: what the user does, what each window does, in what order, roughly how long. |
| c | T16–T18, T70, T71 | Every URL command with its arguments; the full right-click menu in order with its shortcuts; the site's whole keyboard table. |
| d | F14 (T39, T40, T51) | Does a single click on a tile do anything? Does double-clicking the title bar of an already framed window go back? Is a floating window's title bar left to macOS? Does a double-click in the gap between docked windows count as the Desktop? |
| e | F04 (T12–T15) | Strip height, and whether it sits in the slot above the window or over its title bar. What a tab shows. What happens when the tabs do not fit. Reordering by drag. Which tab is selected after the active one closes or is torn off. How far a tab must travel to tear off. How the whole group is dragged. Shortcuts for next and previous tab. The size of the "middle" drop zone against the edge zones. T15 confirmed by trying close and minimize on a group. |
| f | F09 (T08–T11) | The default and range of "Hold over a target", and whether screen edges wait too. Any modifier that suppresses or forces docking. Depth of the screen-edge band and of a window's edge zones (Arbor: 12 points and 30%). The values of `previewStyle`. What "stay pushed" looks like. |
| g | F02 (T42, T43, T53) | The easing of the 460 ms motion, and whether camera moves use it too. On zooming in, do the windows leaving the view fly outward past the screen edges as the whole plane scales, or shrink or fade in place? (F02 says they shrink toward their parent's frame.) What moving windows look like without Screen Recording. |
| h | F10 (T70, T75) | The menu bar buttons from left to right, their glyphs, whether unsaved views get a button, what "filled" looks like. The onboarding steps. |
| i | F16 (T36, T37, T41) | Where between two levels a released pinch settles in rather than out (half-way in scale, best overlap, velocity?). What happens past "everything". Whether two fingers pan without a pinch. |
| j | T20, T21, T31 | The inferred items, tried: a window that cannot shrink to its slot; a single floating window; ⌃⌥↓ with `stepTowardPointer` off and on (also settles Q2). |
| k | F08 (T44, T63) | What happens to a display's arrangement when it is unplugged and plugged back in. Whether each macOS Space has its own arrangement or one is shared. |

- **Answer** (documenter, 2026-10-14), partly:
  - **a** answered: the full settings table is now in
    the reference, "Settings in the app bundle": every key with
    its default, and every option the settings window lists as advanced or
    hidden, with a paraphrased label and the key it most likely sets.
  - **b** partly: the five how-it-works moves are written out step by step in the
    reference ("The five moves"), from the published page. The film has not
    been reviewed (no one has watched it), and the page's simulation has no
    published timings.
  - **c** answered as far as published: the reference now lists everything the
    site publishes (three URL commands plus "and more", three menu items, the
    whole keyboard table). The complete URL and menu lists are only visible in
    the running app; see below.
  - **d–k** need someone to use the running app;
    these are on the board as a task ("Observe TrellisWM").
    Until answers arrive, **the executor decides**: use the numbers already in the
    specs, make each one a setting where it is cheap, and record the choice as
    "Decided by the executor" here.
- **Answer** (documenter, 2026-10-14), d–k partly, from the how-it-works
  simulation watched frame by frame (source **sim** in the reference; a web
  animation of the app, so the running app can still differ):
  - **d**: T39 and T40 confirmed as camera moves. Not shown: a single click on
    a tile, a second title-bar double-click, floating title bars, the gap.
  - **e**: T12, T13. The strip sits in the slot above the window (about 30
    points, estimated; F04's 28 stands) and the window keeps its title bar
    below it; tabs share the width equally; a dot and the title on each tab;
    the selected tab is a lighter chip; dragging the active window's title bar
    moves the whole group; a torn-off tab becomes an ordinary window and a
    group of one loses its strip. Not shown: overflow, reordering, which tab is
    selected after a close, tear-off distance, tab shortcuts, drop-zone sizes, T15.
  - **f**: T11's look (tinted rounded region with a brighter outline, plus the
    region the Desktop keeps). Not shown: hold time, modifiers, band depths,
    the other `previewStyle` values, what "stay pushed" looks like.
  - **g**: T43. Camera moves are a zoom of the whole plane: leaving windows
    slide out past the screen edges at their scaled size; nothing shrinks or
    fades in place; an ease-out with no overshoot. F02 is updated to match.
    Not shown: the duration, and the look without Screen Recording.
  - **i**: T36. Past the root the arrangement keeps shrinking below the
    screen's size, wallpaper around it, while the fingers stay down. Not
    shown: where a release settles, panning without a pinch. F16 is updated.
  - **h**, **j**, **k**: not in the simulation.
  - Also: tiles show the icon with no caption (T50; F15's `tileStyle` covers
    it); a four-window group in the overview showed one tile per window (T51).
  - The rest needs the running app, which needs a person to grant TrellisWM
    Accessibility; until then the executor decides, as above.

## Q11: Should plain ⇧-drag on the Desktop frame a rectangle?

- Asked by: F03/F05 agent (via the documenter), 2026-10-14
- Context: TrellisWM's how-it-works page frames with "hold ⇧ and drag a
  rectangle" (T38), with ⌘⇧-drag anywhere as a hidden option. F05 ships
  ⇧⌘-drag everywhere, because plain ⇧-drag over an app extends selections.
- **Answer** (documenter, 2026-10-14): yes. Plain ⇧-drag that **starts** with no app
  window under the pointer (the Desktop, a gap, a tile) also frames; reuse
  `GestureTap`'s window-under-pointer check. ⇧⌘-drag anywhere stays.
  Setting `frameRectangleOnDesktop` (default true). Board item "F05 follow-up", lane M.

## Q12: Which display, and which window, does a shortcut act on?

- Asked by: swarm product review, 2026-10-14. For the documenter or the maintainer.
- Context: camera commands act on the display under the **pointer**; window commands act on the **focused** window. With two displays, typing on one with the pointer
  resting on the other, ⌃⌥↑ zooms the display you are not looking at, and ⌃⌥↩, pressed next, acts on the other one. The same split is behind Q2 (`in` steps toward a pointer that may
  have been left anywhere).
- Proposal (`docs/features/F56-keys-follow-the-keyboard.md`): gestures and clicks follow the pointer; shortcuts follow the keyboard focus, unless the pointer has moved since the last key press or focus
  change. One setting, Q2's `stepInToward`, with a third value `"auto"` as the default. F33 proposes the same switch under the name `stepTowardPointer`: one key, please.
- **Answer**:

  What if the camera is on one window? Then choose F31's default.

## Q14: What exactly does a window trace hold, and what does a replay check?

- Asked by: executor M, 2026-10-14. **Decided by the executor** (S3 has no spec; the board entry, I7 and Q9 describe it).
- Context: S3 says a trace records `WindowSystem` events and the frames Arbor
  sets, and that a replayer runs it against the real controller. Replaying
  only those lines cannot work: without the command that ran, a replay cannot
  tell why Arbor set a frame, and debounced work (saves, enforcement,
  put-backs) would run at a guessed time rather than where it ran.
- Decisions:
  1. A trace also holds every command (recorded before it runs, so the frames
     it sets follow it; `trace` itself is left out) and every scheduled piece
     of work as it fires, by key. Replays run commands through the controller
     and scheduled work by key, in the recorded order.
  2. The replay's standard is "the same frames in the same order": each
     `setFrame` is answered with what the real app did then, and every frame
     the replayed Arbor sets that the recording does not have at that point,
     has with another frame, or has but the replay never set, is a divergence.
     Focus calls are recorded but not compared.
  3. Traces are written under `$ARBOR_HOME/traces/`; `make trace NAME=x`
     copies one into `collab/evidence/traces/x.jsonl`, and a test replays
     every file there. Titles are never recorded.
  4. `Harness` always carries a recorder with tracing off, so `trace on` works
     in arbor-sim and in tests; the recorder forwards without copying.
- **Answer**: decided as above; say here if any of it should change.
- **Amended** (swarm environment S3, 2026-10-14, which built the same item from the coordinator's brief at the same time and
  merged the two; `docs/features/F28-traces.md`, `docs/TRACES.md`). Decisions 1, 3 and 4 stand. One format survives, the swarm's:
  5. A trace starts with a header that holds what Arbor knew (workspaces, settings, parked windows, learned minimum sizes, the
     filter), not only the windows and displays. Otherwise a trace started in the middle of a session replays from
     "everything floats" and diverges at once.
  6. Titles are replaced by stable tokens rather than dropped, and the title patterns of rules by marks the tokens carry, so title
     rules and re-matching replay. Still no title in any trace.
  7. Also recorded: command results (`ok` and `message` only), overlay actions (a seam drag or a click on a tile is an input
     like any other), the drop preview, every read with its answer (`dock left` acts on the focused window), settings
     as they are applied, and the raw notifications and mouse events under the events.
  8. Decision 2 becomes **strict** mode, which also compares `focus`, the drop preview and command results. Committed traces are replayed in
     **free** mode, where only invariants are checked: a real trace cannot be re-recorded by whoever changes Arbor's behaviour on
     purpose, so it must not fail for that.
  9. Limits: 20 MB or 10 minutes, then it stops by itself. Drag moves are thinned to about twenty a second, never the last of a
     run or one Arbor reacted to. The clock is monotonic. Kinds of line a reader does not know are skipped, not fatal.
  10. `make scenarios` records and strictly replays each scenario, so that a new way across the seams cannot go unrecorded unnoticed.

## Q15: What does "show everything" look like: the real windows, or a picture of them?

- Asked by: documenter, 2026-10-14 (issue #2, decision 1). The documenter answered provisionally there.
- Context: the reference draws its overview itself: a darkened live picture of each window with icon and title, the real windows out of view
  meanwhile. Arbor lays the real windows out smaller, and shows a tile for one that cannot be that small.
- **Answer** (product owner, 2026-10-14): **the real windows. The provisional answer stands and is provisional no longer.**
  1. It needs no second permission. Pictures need Screen Recording: one more prompt in the first minute, which macOS repeats from time to time.
  2. What shows is the window itself. It can be read, clicked and typed in at once, it is never stale, and nothing has to hand over to it, so nothing can jump (#3, rule 3).
  3. A drawn overview is visual parity, and nothing in a working day waits for it (#3, rule 7).
- What follows from it:
  - **A picture overview is not on the board.** It is IDEAS I13. It may be proposed again once `docs/VIABLE.md` has passed, as an option for people who have granted Screen Recording already.
  - **Arbor never asks for Screen Recording by itself**, and `windowPictures` stays false. So colour and icon is the look that ships, and F02's second step and F16 are judged
    **in that look first**. Pictures are a second look, judged on hosted Macs by the same rubric; when they pass they may switch themselves on where the permission is already there.
  - **Two windows of one app can be told apart in the overview without a click.** Played on a 1440-point display with four docked apps at their real smallest sizes: two are tiles,
    63 and 157 points wide, and the tile of one Safari window says nothing that tells it from the other Safari window. So `tileStyle` ships as `"name"`, and a window's tile is
    captioned with the space's name if it has one, else the window's title, else the app's name. A tile too narrow for eight letters shows the icon alone; every tile has its caption as its help tag.
    While the view is held, at either strength, the app's name stands in for the title, as on F04's strips.
  - A title is drawn and nothing else: not logged, not in a trace, not in `state` or `watch` without `--titles`.

## Q16: What does Arbor do to the windows that are open when it starts?

- Asked by: documenter, 2026-10-14 (issue #2, decision 2; #3, rule 2).
- Context: the reference arranges and resizes every window on the desktop the moment it starts, which surprised the person trying it. Arbor takes them in as floating and waits.
- **Answer** (product owner, 2026-10-14): **nothing. Arbor's way stays, as a deliberate difference, and `docs/GUIDE.md` says so.**
  1. It was the first thing that went wrong for the tester, before any feature had a chance (#3).
  2. An arrangement is worth something because its owner made it. Arbor cannot know which three of twelve windows matter.
  3. The way in costs one drag or one key, and the first-run coach invites it (F46).
- What follows from it:
  - `newWindows` stays `"float"`. Nothing docks that nobody docked.
  - **A later start** brings back what the person had docked, each window moving once, because that arrangement is theirs (T60). One rule for the view: **a start takes no window out of view that is in view when Arbor starts.**
    **When a person starts Arbor**, whatever ended the last run (a login item's start and the start after an update are such starts too: an update quits Arbor first), it ends showing everything, which also fetches what a crash left in the corner; the view it stopped in goes on the history, so ⌃⌥[ returns to it, and saved views, names and older history stay.
    **When the watchdog restarts it** a second after a crash, nobody quit and every window is where it was: arrangement and view are kept and no frame is set. A start never sizes a floating window or sends one away: a window that summon had sent away is an
    ordinary floating window at a person's start. A display that is gone at the start and plugged in later, or was dormant and is back, comes back showing everything too. A resume after a pause or a returned permission keeps the view, and so does an unplug and replug within one run.
    The watchdog's restart keeps the view only if its first layout would send away nothing that is in view now (what was saved can be half a second behind); otherwise that display shows everything.
  - **If the keyboard is in a docked window that would be a tile at everything** (it fits nowhere but alone): that window is let be where it stands, docked in the tree, its place reserved, the keyboard in it, until the next command that lays out or frames it (the state of Q22). Not "it is framed and the rest goes", which is the fault again by another road;
    not "the keyboard goes to a neighbour", which nobody asked for (rule 4). A follow-up: the first fix ships with this as a known fault of its own name.
  - **At a start a waiting place keeps its room**: a docked window that is restored before its neighbour goes straight to its final place, and a hole waits for the neighbour. If the wait ends in vain, the room goes back at the next layout the person causes: nothing moves by a timer.
  - Played on the trunk (`arbor-sim replay --frames`: three windows, two docked, one framed, `quit`, `launch`): the first start moves nothing, and quitting gives all three their own frames back.
    The second start frames the window that was framed and sends the other two to the corner, one of them a window that was never docked. **That is a fault against rule 2**: on the board.

## Q17: Does anything of Arbor's lie between the pointer and the windows during a drag?

- Asked by: documenter, 2026-10-14 (issue #2, decision 3; #3, rule 6).
- Context: the reference covers the screen with one window of its own during a drag. Accessibility tools and automation cannot let go of a drag over a foreign full-screen window.
- **Answer** (product owner, 2026-10-14): **no. The preview stays a panel that ignores the mouse, and there will be no drop layer.**
  1. Rule 6: whoever cannot let go of a window has lost the Mac, not a feature.
  2. Read on the trunk: the preview, the stage, the rectangle's band, the focus ring, the surface and the line on screen all ignore the mouse. What takes clicks (tiles, gap handles, the peek's outline,
     the minimap when asked, the panels with a field) is no larger than what it stands for.
  3. Reading is not proof, and the next panel will be written by somebody who has not read this. So it becomes a rule with checks.
- **The rule.** While a mouse button is down that went down outside Arbor's own panels, Arbor puts no panel that takes the mouse under the pointer, and moves none there. No panel that takes the mouse
  covers a whole display, except the peek's outline while the person holds its keys.
- **The checks** (board, "Nothing of Arbor's takes the mouse-up"): on Linux, one table of Arbor's panels (level, takes the mouse or not, why), and a check that fails for a panel that is not in it. On a hosted Mac, in `e2e-drag.sh`,
  at both settings of macOS tiling, for every drag: the button is up within 100 ms of the posted mouse-up (50 at first; a Mac without Arbor broke that once in 118), the window ends where the preview was, and a click at that point 300 ms later arrives in the window that lies there.

## Q18: Who says that something may ship switched on, now that nobody is asked to look?

- Asked by: product owner, 2026-10-14. Issue #4 forbids what the rule so far rested on ("ships off until a person has tried it on a Mac", under "what ships off" below).
- **Answer** (product owner, 2026-10-14): **the loop does.** A thing ships switched on when all of these hold on one commit:
  1. its own script has run unattended on a hosted Mac, in under ten minutes, at both settings of macOS tiling, and every check in it passed;
  2. the detectors of `docs/VIABLE.md` found nothing in what that run recorded;
  3. if it holds or swallows input (an event tap, a panel that takes the mouse, a registered key): every key, click and scroll that is not Arbor's reached the witness, none more than 10 ms later than without Arbor;
  4. if it draws: its rows of the polish rubric pass, in light and dark.
- What a hosted Mac cannot produce (a real trackpad, a camera or a call, a lid, a notch, a second physical display, a login) proves nothing either way. A feature whose safety rests on one of those is **redesigned until it does not**, or
  ships off and is called an experiment in the guide. It is not done, and the board says so.
- The caution that rule came from stays: compare the known faults of what is in with the risks of what would replace it, and weigh the failure nobody has thought of. A click that vanishes looks like a broken Mac.

## Q19: What decides a release?

- Asked by: product owner, 2026-10-14. Issue #3 ends with half an hour on one person's ordinary Mac; issue #4, which is later, says that quality is never judged by asking the maintainer to look.
- **Answer** (product owner, 2026-10-14): **`docs/VIABLE.md`.** The half hour becomes the day that a script plays with real apps on hosted Macs at macOS's default settings, many times over, judged by detectors. Its measure is #3's: no jolt, no lost or stuck
  window, nothing moved that the person did not move. Anybody may still try Arbor and say what they saw, and what they say becomes a test; nothing waits for it.
- A tag still needs what it needed: nothing of severity 1 or 2 known that can be met with the settings as they ship. "New since `main`" no longer excuses an old one.

## Q20: What may Arbor do to a floating window that nobody touched?

- Asked by: the coordinator, 2026-10-14, after the first day played with nine real apps on a hosted Mac at macOS's defaults. A dock squeezed floating windows nobody had touched (one from 1324 to 794 points wide). ⌘H, ⌘M and ⌘Q on a docked window moved floating
  windows too, and unhiding did not undo it. After framing a window and showing everything, floating windows came back somewhere else than they had been.
- Context: rule 2 says that nothing moves which the person did not move. The specs on room, the Desktop and the squeeze (F18, F48, F61, scenario 20) say that floating windows are kept on the Desktop. Both cannot hold.
  Replayed on the trunk in arbor-sim: four windows, two docks, and a mail window that nobody touched goes from 1000 to 714 to 464 points wide.
- **Answer** (product owner, 2026-10-14): **a floating window's frame is its person's. Arbor never resizes it and never moves it on screen.**
  1. Shoving and squeezing every other window at the first dock is the very thing issue #3 complains of in the reference.
  2. The guide has promised all along that other windows "float exactly as they always have".
  3. Nearly every lost window found so far came from Arbor placing or squeezing a floating window. This removes the family instead of mending its members.
- **The one thing Arbor does to a floating window:** it takes it out of view when the view leaves the Desktop, and puts it back exactly where it was, same place, same size, one move, when the Desktop is in view again.
- **The one exception**, because "never lost" outranks "never moved": if the displays have changed meanwhile and that frame would show less than half of the window, it is fitted: moved the least that brings it wholly into view, and made smaller only if it is larger than the display.
  As it is read by whoever built it (2026-10-15): "the displays have changed" means that some display's frame is another, or that one came or went; the Dock and the menu bar do not count. Fitted is every floating window in view of which less than half shows on all displays together: one move. **What stays is a window the change did not touch**: as much of it shows as before, and the display that shows most of it is as large as it was, or the window's app has not spoken of it within the last half minute (only then can "before" have been measured too late); whoever put it there: a hand, its app, a tool. A display that only grows pulls in nothing. And there and back is nothing: a window the fit made smaller goes back to its own frame, one move, when the display is restored. So a lid that moves every corner, the Dock, the menu bar, or another display that comes, goes or shrinks fits nothing that lost nothing. It is geometry only, with no note of the hand to go stale,
  and it moves fewer windows. (A first wording went by where the hand had left the window.) The price, accepted: if its own display gets another size and less than half shows, a window is pulled wholly in, even if the change took nothing from it. This is only about when a change of the displays makes Arbor act at all: where Arbor itself places a window, an overhang is honoured only for a frame its person gave, on this display as it is shaped now. Where in doubt: wholly in view.
- Floating and docked windows may overlap, as windows on a Mac do. macOS's order says which is in front; the window acted on or switched to comes to the front (rule 4); ⌃⌥D shows everything that floats.
- **What a person sees at their first dock.** The window docked is the one in the hand or with the keyboard, so it is in front; loose windows that lay there are behind it, untouched. It looks like macOS's own tiling of one window, which people know.
  A window docked from the menu or by command while another is in front is raised too. **Arbor raises nothing else**, not the other docked windows, not at a dock and not at a move of the view: raising the arrangement would bury a call that somebody keeps in front. Taking a window out of view and
  bringing it back does not change its place in the order. What says that something happened: the surface behind docked windows, the line round the focused one, "Docked · ⌃⌥↩ frames it". The coach goes straight on to Frame, where the whole heap leaves the view, and to Show Everything, where it is back exactly:
  that pair is where Arbor pays.
- **The price, known and accepted:** at Show Everything loose windows may lie over docked ones. (They did before: on the hosted day four floating windows overlapped a docked one by some 400 points for most of the day, because their apps refused the squeeze.) For whoever wants them gathered: IDEAS I14.
- **There and back is nothing.** Frame and Show Everything; hide and unhide; minimize and restore: when nothing else has changed between, every window is exactly where it was, the docked neighbours at their old sizes.
  **The gaps depend on who is away, never on the order.** The arrangement is the person's as of their last change to it (a gap or an edge dragged, a window docked, floated, closed or swapped, arrange, layout, undo). What Arbor lends and borrows while windows are away (F61) is worked out afresh from those gaps each time,
  so the same set of windows always gives the same frames. A move of the view and a switch of macOS Space change nothing. Not kept across a restart of Arbor.
- How it is built: a setting `floatingWindowsMakeWay`, which ships **false**. True is the behaviour so far, so that the tests written for it keep their meaning and the decision can be undone. With false there is no placing and no squeeze, a window that floats alone is not stretched over the
  Desktop (F18), and F61 counts the Desktop as not in use: docked windows come first.
- Overruled, with the setting as it ships: "floating windows make way" (scenario 20), "a window that floats alone takes the Desktop" (F18), the squeeze and all that follows from it (own sizes of squeezed windows in F48, F49, F53), "what Arbor took from the Desktop it gives back while a window floats on it" (F61).

## Q21: What does a pause do to the windows in view?

- Asked by: a fixer, 2026-10-14. The hosted day found that a pause gives no size back: docked windows keep their slots' sizes. F40 says "what you are looking at stays as it is"; rule 5 says a pause gives windows their own earlier sizes back.
- **Answer** (product owner, 2026-10-14): **rule 5 wins, and a pause has one meaning: as if Arbor had quit, and it comes back.** Every window Arbor sized goes to its own earlier frame by the road Quit takes, one move each, nothing raised. Resume brings arrangement and view back, one move each.
  The pause Arbor begins by itself for an app in `pauseForApps` is the same pause: one meaning is worth more than a special case in a setting that ships empty. Whoever only wants ⌃⌥ back has Shortcuts Off in “App” (F41), which moves nothing; the guide says so beside pause.
- Built (board 1.20). `pause` now replies "…; windows are back in view, in their own sizes". While floating windows still make way (Q20), a resume also puts back on the Desktop those the pause took off it. What is left is in F40's "Not shipped".

## Q22: macOS zooms a docked window at a double-click on its title bar. What does Arbor do?

- Asked by: the porter of F14, 2026-10-14. With both of Arbor's double-click switches off, macOS zooms or fills the window and Arbor pulls it back a quarter of a second later: two moves, and a fight (rules 1 and 3).
- **Answer** (product owner, 2026-10-14): **Arbor stands aside.** macOS's frame stays, the window stays docked, its place stays reserved, nobody else moves. It goes back in one move at macOS's own second double-click, or at the next command that lays out.
  Not "it floats and the neighbours close up": that moves windows nobody touched. The first three times a line on screen says how to get back (F43), if that is cheap.

## Q23: With macOS's own tiling on, whose are the screen's edges?

- Asked by: product owner, 2026-10-14, reading the change that makes Arbor stand aside where macOS tiles (board 1.1).
- Context: measured on a hosted Mac, macOS takes a dragged window only in the outermost point of the left and right edges and in the menu bar. Arbor's own band along the screen's edges is 12 points deep and previews at once. A real pointer travels:
  on its way to the last point it crosses the band, so Arbor's preview comes up, goes again, and macOS's follows. People push the pointer against the edge, so nobody can let go within the band on purpose.
- **Answer** (product owner, 2026-10-14): **while macOS's switch for an edge is on, that whole edge is macOS's.** With "drag to the left or right edge to tile" on, Arbor offers nothing in its left and right bands; with "drag to the menu bar to fill" on, nothing in its top band. No preview, no dock:
  a **floating** window let go there, short of macOS's own point, floats where it is. **A docked window let go there belongs to whoever takes it** (added 2026-10-15; it was written nowhere, and the code differed by edge): if macOS takes it (tiles or fills), it is macOS's: undocked, its place closes, Arbor sets no frame; if macOS does not, nothing was offered and nothing happens: it goes home to its slot in one move, its neighbours untouched. The same at the left, the right and the top, and in the corners.
  Arbor cannot know at the release which it will be, so it looks at where the window server has the window about half a second after the release (macOS's own move takes 0.30 to 0.34 s, and an app's late report of the drag's last move must not pass for it). The price, accepted: when macOS does take the window, the neighbours close the gap half a second later than they used to.
  1. Rule 1 says that Arbor stands aside at the top edge and the side edges "rather than both reacting to one drag". Two previews one after the other are both reacting.
  2. A preview that shows for a few frames is a flicker, which the rubric fails.
  3. One sentence can be remembered: "With macOS's tiling on, the left, right and top edges are macOS's." The bottom edge, docked windows' own edges and the keys stay Arbor's.
- What follows: at macOS's defaults a drag to the left edge does not dock. So the first page of the welcome window says so and offers the fix in one step, which is seen to have worked; declining is respected. **The coach never teaches a move that cannot work on this Mac as it is set**: with macOS's tiling on, its first
  move is ⌃⌥⌘←, which works at every setting. The line on screen after such a drop names the key that docks at once.
- For later: IDEAS I15.

## Q24: macOS sweeps the windows aside at a click on the wallpaper. What does Arbor do meanwhile?

- Asked by: the fixer of board 1.5, 2026-10-14, after a probe on a hosted Mac (22 sweeps). The product owner's first design was "Arbor notices from the window list, moves nothing while they are swept, and takes no swept frame for the person's".
- What the Mac answered: a sweep is only a picture. Accessibility keeps every true frame, while the window list shows the swept ones. A frame set through Accessibility meanwhile is taken, stays invisible, does not end the sweep, and the window comes back at the new frame in one motion.
  Arbor ended all ten cases tried rightly (idle, close, dock, zoom, focus, pause, Quit). The Dock announces the beginning and the end, 0.2 s late. **The real fault:** a click on the strip of a swept window, the natural way back, that slips by 3 points: the window list jumps back while the button is
  down, and Arbor takes that for a drag by hand. Strips lie at the display's edges, which are drop zones, so a mere click can dock a floating window.
- **Answer** (product owner, 2026-10-14): **while the windows are swept Arbor goes by Accessibility only, never by the window list.** No drag begins from a press on a swept window: no preview, no "moved by the user". No stage goes up over the bare desktop: a layout made meanwhile takes its frames unseen. Shortcuts keep working. (Struck on 2026-10-15: "the pause's rescues ignore swept frames". Both rescues go after windows that truly are out of view, and fetching those is right and unseen, swept or not; no honest failing test could be written for the sentence, so it is no requirement.)
  "Moves nothing while swept" is withdrawn: putting moves off would make a visible jolt after the windows are back, and moving meanwhile shows nothing.
- What is Arbor's own (the line round the focused window, tiles, gap handles, the surface) goes with the windows and comes back with them.

## Q25: A window kept in view lies behind the window that has the keyboard. What does "Keep in View" promise?

- Asked by: the coordinator, 2026-10-14, from the real-window suite on hosted Macs. Pin a call, frame the editor: the call is wholly covered, while the menu's tick and `state` say it is kept in view. Arbor sees the cover and raises three times after every event, in vain:
  the Accessibility raise lifts a background app's window over everything but the active app's key window. No public call is known that does more without taking the keyboard.
- The choices: (a) a private window-server call; (b) place instead of raise; (c) take the promise back and say what is true; (d) activate the pinned app for a moment and give the keyboard back.
- **Answer** (product owner, 2026-10-14): **(b), with (c)'s honesty where (b) has no room. Keep in View makes room instead of fighting for the front.**
  1. Not (a): a private call under the one promise people lean on during a call is a promise that breaks with an update of macOS.
  2. Not (d): it takes the keyboard, however briefly (rules 4 and 6).
  3. A call beside the editor, neither covering the other, is what whoever pins a call wants (rule 7).
- **The rule.** A window kept in view reserves a strip of its display, as the Dock does: from its inner edge to the display's edge, over the whole height or width; of the four strips, the one that costs the least area. Every view, a framed window, a group, everything, is laid out in what is left.
  Once pinned, the window itself is never moved or sized (Q20); a docked window gets its own earlier size as it is pinned. Docked windows move once when the pin is set, when it is taken off, and when the person lets go after dragging or resizing the pinned window.
- **Where there is no room:** if the cheapest strip would cost more than 40 % of the view (a pin in the middle, or a very large one), none is reserved. The window still never goes out of view, and Arbor says so: a line on screen at the pin, and the menu's item and `state` say "covered" when it is.
  Two pins each have their strip while the sum stays under 40 %, in the order pinned.
- **The raise:** one, when the pin is set. No retries. By Q20 Arbor raises nothing else: the raise of windows left hanging over docked ones goes; a guest that comes back and a summoned window that stays follow the pin's rule.
- Built, 2026-10-15, of the last point: the raise of windows left hanging over docked ones is gone (K087 below is overruled), and so is the raise of whatever came back into view at its own frame: a guest, a floating window while the Desktop is a tile.
  As it is read by whoever built it, and confirmed by the product owner the same day: **Arbor never raises a guest.** It has no moment at which it is set but its opening, when macOS itself puts it in front; "one raise as it comes back" is struck. What is left of `raise` in the controller is the pin's own, retries included, which also serves a summoned window: it goes with board 1.25.
  A pinned or summoned window that comes back from out of view is still raised as it comes: pinned while it was out of view, that is the raise at the pin.
  **The price, accepted (product owner, 2026-10-15; the measure: what would the Mac do without Arbor?):** with the Desktop a tile, docked windows fill the display, so after Show Everything a floating window may lie wholly behind them; a guest may lie wholly behind the host that was switched to. Each is a switch, Go to…, ⌃⌥D or a click on the tile away.
  The walks hold Arbor to it: `raised-unasked` speaks at any raise of a window that is neither kept in view nor summoned.

## Q26: What of the things that ship switched on and have never run on a Mac?

- Asked by: product owner, 2026-10-15, on reading `docs/CENSUS.md`, table 4. Q18 says when a thing may be switched on. It says nothing of what was switched on before anybody asked: the event taps (F55's has never run; macOS holds every scroll, gesture and press of the whole Mac until Arbor's callback returns), a hold of ⌃⌥Space (F51),
  the permission taken away with taps installed (F63), displays and Spaces (F08), pause by its key, the stage, which never plays in CI because hosted Macs reduce motion. CI's real-window job has never pressed a key.
- **Answer** (product owner, 2026-10-15): **a deadline, not a pardon.**
  1. The census's table 4 is the queue for Mac runs at the first priority, from the top. The scripts exist; they need running and reading.
  2. **No build is handed to a person while rank 1, 2 or 3 says "never".** What has not passed when a build is cut ships off in that build: `scrollZoom` false; by the result of `input-taps.sh`, `gesturesEnabled` false, `frameRectangleModifier` none and `frameRectangleOnDesktop` false (with the last one true the rectangle's taps stay installed: what is meant is no event tap at all at launch); by that of `check-F51.sh`, `peek` false, so that ⌃⌥Space is plain Show Everything.
     Each is a default of one line, flipped back by a commit of its own when its script is green. A click that vanishes looks like a broken Mac.
  3. From rank 4 down the deadline is the first attempt at the gate: then on and never run means off.
- The census's column "Ever run?" is kept current, since it now decides defaults.

## Q27: A default is changed. Whom does the change reach?

- Asked by: the keeper of the census's queue, 2026-10-15, reading the code before cutting Q26's flips. The first launch writes every default into `settings.json`. So a changed default reaches only a Mac on which Arbor has never run: whoever has run an earlier build keeps the old values, and whoever starts tonight's build first has tonight's values written down for good. A written default cannot be told from a choice.
- **Answer** (product owner, 2026-10-15), in two parts.
  1. **For Q26, at once: one new key**, absent from every file in the world, which is asked before the four: `pointerGestures`; false means that Arbor installs no event tap, whatever the four say. It replaces the four flips: one line to land, one to flip back. "No tap at launch" has to hold on a Mac that has run Arbor before, since that is where a first build is most likely to go.
     It is worth having for good: the plain master switch above the four in the settings window.
  2. **For the family, after the first build, with care: `settings.json` holds only what differs from the defaults.** Otherwise every flip to come is swallowed (`tileStyle`, `tabsEnabled`, `continuousZoom`). For files already written there is one migration, once: a file without the new mark is stripped of every value that equals the default as it was when such files were written (a frozen table), and is then marked.
     Whoever had chosen a value that happened to be the default loses nothing that day and follows the default from then on: an honest price before a first release, and the notes say so. Hand-edited keys, their order and unknown keys survive as they do now.

## Q28: Two windows are docked, left and right. How much of the screen does each get?

- Asked by: the keeper of the first run, 2026-10-15, who played the coach's sentences in arbor-sim and found three untrue. Dock left gives a half, beside the Desktop. Dock a second right gives **thirds**: window, Desktop, window; and the first window shrinks from a half to a third.
- **Answer** (product owner, 2026-10-15): **two docks are two halves. The Desktop is what is left over, not a place with a share of its own.**
  1. "Side by side" means halves to every newcomer, and side by side is the first half of what Arbor is for.
  2. On a display 1440 points wide a third is 464 points, which Safari (574), Notes (709) and Mail (760) all refuse: tiles at the second dock.
  3. Since Q20 loose windows are not kept in the middle, so a third of the screen is reserved for nobody. With halves they lie behind the two just docked, and the screen is tidy at the second move.
- **The rule.** A window that is docked takes its room from what is left first, all of it if need be; docked windows give only when nothing is left. So the second dock moves one window, and the first stays as it is (rule 2). With nothing left the Desktop has no width, no tile and no gap. ⌃⌥D, the menu and the menu bar still go there.
  Room comes back when a docked window leaves, to where it was taken from; or when a person drags a docked window's inner edge inward.
- How it is built: after Q20's change has landed; a setting keeps today's thirds, so that the tests written for them keep their meaning.

## Q29: The first person has used Arbor for their work. What does Arbor do by default?

- Asked by: the maintainer, 2026-10-14, as IDEAS I16 to I23, written after working with the first build. One theme in eight notes: the person wants to stay in charge, and Arbor to step in only when asked.
- **Answer** (product owner, 2026-10-15): **less. Arbor does nothing that was not asked for, and what was asked for touches only the windows it names.** The eight notes are direction, not suggestions: #3, rule 7, ranks daily use above likeness to the reference, and this is what daily use said. Everything below is the default of the second build. What goes off stays in the app as an option.
- **The test for a default, from now on:** would a person who has read nothing be surprised that it happened, or have to learn a word to undo it? Then it is off until they ask.
  1. **Nine keys, each ⌃⌥ and one key (I22).** ⌃⌥← and ⌃⌥→ dock the window in front at that side, or move it one place. ⌃⌥↩ fills the screen with it; again, and it is back. ⌃⌥Space shows everything. ⌃⌥[ goes back. ⌃⌥Z undoes. ⌃⌥F floats a window, or takes it back in. ⌃⌥G is Go to…. ⌃⌥⎋ pauses and resumes.
     Nothing else is bound: no third modifier, no digits, no other letter. The rest is in the menu and in Go to…, and in the README's recipes for `bindings`. One setting brings back the whole map as it was, less ⌃⌥A, so that whoever wants the reference's table has it in one step, and the scripts and tests that press those keys keep their meaning.
     The arrows dock because docking is what a newcomer came for, because it is what ⌃⌥ and an arrow do in the window tools people already know, and because looking about needs no key of its own while little goes out of view.
  2. **⌃⌥A is never bound again (I16).** With a widely used chat app in front it archived the open conversation there, twice. Whatever the cause turns out to be, the habit outlives Arbor: paused, quit or crashed, the same fingers reach the app. A key that destroys or hides something elsewhere is worse than no key. Arrange Windows is the first item of the menu.
     **A cause has been seen on a hosted Mac, on the trunk and on the first build's own binary: another build signed ad hoc over a grant made for the old one.** The entry in System Settings still says "allowed", Arbor waits for the permission, its item is in the menu bar as a small triangle, and it holds no key: ⌃⌥A reached the app in front, twice in a row. **A second candidate, shown with posted events only: with the flag of the fn key on the event, Arbor's letter keys are not Arbor's.** Arbor running, all keys registered, not paused: seven of seven reach the app in front and Arbor's log does not grow: the window server compares that flag exactly, and on a laptop fn sits beside ⌃. That a real fn key sets the flag there is not shown, and nobody is asked to try. Both are cured: each key is registered a second time with that bit, if a hosted Mac shows that such a registration hears it. The record says "likeliest", never "the" cause: nobody has read the person's log and nobody will ask. What had been asked for: the cause found before the second build is cut, or each of three guesses tried on a hosted Mac and written down: the key was never registered; it was let go of meanwhile (a pause, an app with shortcuts off, secure input, a wake); the app listens below the point where macOS takes a hotkey away, so that both ran. **A key of Arbor's that is not Arbor's at that moment is something Arbor knows and says**: in Check Setup, in `doctor`, in the log.
     Read in the code since: alive and with its item in the menu bar, Arbor held no key at all while it waited for the permission (a first launch; a new build signed ad hoc over an old grant), none after the permission was lost, and only ⌃⌥⎋ in a pause. **So, in its fixer's words: while Arbor's item is in the menu bar, a key of the map in effect never reaches another app, unless the person said so themselves**: for an app, for all, or by pausing Arbor. Waiting for the permission, the permission lost, a false alarm, waiting at a start: nobody said anything: Arbor keeps its keys, takes the press, does nothing, and says why in one line with the way out. That overturns F63's "gives the shortcuts back to the apps". **A pause gives them back, as before** (F40): for twenty minutes I had ruled otherwise. The maintainer's own note counts "not paused" among the conditions under which the key should not have reached the app, so that is what a pause means to the first person who used it; and a paused app that swallows nine chords is in the way. The line at the pause says so: "Arbor is paused: its keys are the apps' again · ⌃⌥⎋ resumes". That a key let through in a pause does no harm is what the table of clashes is for. **Four edges, found by a reader who ran it, decided the same way: who said what?** (a) The pause Arbor takes by itself when a logout begins: nobody said anything, so the keys stay Arbor's; a press moves nothing and says "Arbor is paused · ⌃⌥⎋ resumes"; a pause by hand on top of it gives them away as any pause does. (b) `pauseForApps` is the person's word also while Arbor can pause for nobody (waiting, the permission lost, the moment of a start): with such an app in front the keys are the app's, as for `passThroughApps`. (c) The permission goes, or is doubted, during a pause by hand: the keys they gave away stay given away until Arbor runs again: no key goes dead in a game behind their back. And when does it run again? A loss that a person mends in System Settings ends the pause, as F63's criterion 7 says: granting it again means wanting Arbor. **A doubt that passes by itself ends nothing: nobody granted anything, so the pause stands as it was.** (d) With `messages` off nothing is written on the screen, by the person's own setting: a key that is taken beeps once, a pause says nothing. A key that needs no permission (Settings…) goes on working without it, so the words are "moves nothing", not "does nothing".
     Also seen: an app's own global hot key on the same chord runs as well as Arbor's, both act; macOS never refuses a registration; another process's exclusive hot key takes the key from Arbor without Arbor knowing. So "known and said" has one case macOS does not tell, and `doctor` and `docs/KEYBOARD.md` say that plainly; and the table of clashes needs the apps' published lists of shortcuts besides their menus.
     And the maintainer's second half as written: Arbor reads the menu bar of the app in front and lists each of its own keys that the app also uses. Run over the apps the sizes are measured on, it gives `docs/KEYBOARD.md` a table. **A letter stays among the nine only while no app in that table binds it to something that destroys or hides.**
  3. **A dragged window floats (I20 b).** In a plain drag nothing of Arbor's is on screen: no preview, no waiting over a target, no window making room; letting go docks nothing. **With Arbor running, a plain drag of a floating window is what it is without Arbor.** ⌘ held during the drag asks Arbor: the previews come when the key goes down and go when it comes up, and letting go with it held docks. That is F09 turned round. A setting has the three ways: with the key, always, never.
     A docked window dragged plainly comes out: it floats at its own size where it was let go, and its place closes.
  4. **A dock touches what is docked, and nothing else (I20 c).** Q20, which is being built, is the first half. The second goes further than Q20: **Arbor never takes a floating window out of view.** "The one thing Arbor does to a floating window" is withdrawn. A floating window is moved, sized, raised or hidden only by a command that names it.
  5. **No Desktop column (I20 a).** Q28 goes the same way and now the whole way: two docks are two halves, and Arrange Windows leaves nothing over. By a new key, absent from every file: a file in which somebody set the old width to its floor holds that as a choice, and would keep a column its person asked to be rid of.
  6. **Nothing is drawn that was not asked for (I20 d, I20 e, I23).** The focus ring ships off. The menu bar has one icon. The wallpaper is not darkened behind docked windows, and no hairline goes round the Desktop: neither was named by the maintainer, and both fail the test above (found by the keeper of the gallery, who held the rule against everything Arbor draws). The handle in a gap stays: it shows only under the pointer, and a hand that went there is asking. **Windows go to their places at once: no motion, so nothing stands in for a window.** What was wanted is the window itself; a card with the app's icon is not that, and a picture of the window needs a permission Arbor does not ask for by itself (Q15 stands). "At once is what every window tool on the Mac does", I wrote here, without having looked: it is true of other people's tools and false of macOS's own tiling, which is the bar. Filmed on a hosted Mac: there the real window glides and grows, in one piece, eased, for 283 to 300 ms, 16 to 18 frames, none skipped; of two windows the one in front glides and the other jumps. And Arbor without motion is not yet in one piece (board 0.18). At once still ships in the second build, since cards are what was disliked; whether Arbor can glide the one window a person named, through Accessibility, as macOS does, is being probed, and would be "the window itself" without a permission. The motion stays as an option: whoever turns it on gets pictures of the windows if they grant the permission themselves, by a button that opens System Settings, else cards. It cannot come back as a default by pictures, since nobody has granted that permission on the first day; the only road to a default is the real window moved in steps, which is to be probed.
  7. **What a person asks macOS for stays (I19).** A double-click on a docked window's title bar: what macOS makes of it is let be. No frame is set; the window is still docked and its place is kept; the second double-click, or the next change of the arrangement that the person causes, takes it back in one move.
  8. **Every open window is easy to find (I17).** Three things at once: nothing goes out of view by itself (4 above; a start, a resume, a change of displays and a window that closes send nothing away: board 1.29, 1.41, 1.47); fewer windows are tiles, since a half is wider than a third; and **the menu lists every open window**, by app and title, with a mark for what is out of view, and a click brings it to the front and into view.
     A trial I had put here as perhaps the real answer, "out of view could mean behind, not away", **leaves the second build.** I had written that neither Mission Control nor a glance finds a window at the display's corner, without a picture. A hosted Mac has shown otherwise: with four windows out of view Mission Control and the app's windows (⌃↓) show all five, whole, with title bar and content, and a pick there lands in one move each. And the maintainer's own note names windows "behind the framed space" among those that are hard to find: behind would not have answered them. The way a Mac user already knows works. What is left of the trial is engineering (nothing moved twice, nothing to spill when a display goes, little owed at a Quit), for later.
     An overview to pick from is a feature of its own, together with the next point. Q15 stands: the real windows, no second permission.
  9. **A few windows into one group, in one step (I18).** In Go to…: tick two to four windows, ↩, and they are docked side by side in the order ticked and fill the screen. Nothing else moves. First of the features after the defaults.
  10. **"It would not dock on the right" (I21)** goes to a hunter with a hosted Mac: by key, by drag and by ⌘-drag, with a browser at its real smallest size and another window minimized, floating and docked. A cause has since been proven on Linux: Arrange of three makes a column of two; one of the two minimized, the key "steps out of a group" nobody sees: the same picture, the reply "moved". A key acts on what a person sees: one press, one visible step. A second road: with macOS's tiling on, the right edge was macOS's (Q23). Nobody asks for a trace from a person's Mac.
  11. **The first minute asks for the one permission and for nothing else.** Since plain drags float, Arbor does not need macOS's edges: macOS's tiling stays as the person has it, and is not spoken of in the welcome window. The step that switches it off, and puts it back while Arbor does not arrange windows, stays as built: in Check Setup, and offered on screen once when a ⌘-drag to an edge was taken by macOS. The coach's moves are keys: ⌃⌥←, ⌃⌥→ on a second window, ⌃⌥↩, ⌃⌥↩.
  12. **The new defaults reach a Mac that has run Arbor (Q27).** The settings file that holds only what differs lands before any of them. What a person set by hand stays theirs; the notes name the two bands somebody may have set to nothing as a workaround for 3, since with them at nothing a ⌘-drag finds no target.
- **What this overturns, plainly.**
  - `docs/KEYBOARD.md`, rules 1 to 4 (the reference's table unchanged; ⌃⌥ looks; ⌘ moves; ⇧ is the counterpart): they now describe the whole map, which is an option.
  - Q20's one exception. Q28's "the Desktop is what is left over" stays and Arrange follows it. Q23 stands, and now speaks only of ⌘-drags. Board 1.52 (the first act on a fresh Mac is the one macOS takes away) dissolves: the first act is a key. IDEAS I15 falls behind.
  - Q15's "colour and icon is the look that ships": the look that ships has no motion. The order of section 3 of the board: the motion's second step is no longer first of the polish.
  - Answers of mine from the same night: the welcome window's button in the first minute, and the film's shot of it; "in macOS's band, not taken: home" (board 1.56): **a plain drag means out, wherever it ends**: macOS takes the window, or it floats at its own size where it lies. At macOS's own point (the last point of a side, the menu bar, ⌥ held) that holds with ⌘ held too: whoever carries a window there asked macOS. Home is for a ⌘-drag that found no target, in macOS's band short of its point included, and for a slip.
  - The coach's moves by drag; the film's opening, its cards, ring and buttons; the claims of the suite on real windows that dock by a plain drag; the journeys of `docs/VIABLE.md`, which run at the switches that ship; the polish rubric where it scores the ring and the cards as shipped.
- **Held against the maintainer's handoff of the same night** (`collab/HANDOFF-2026-10-14-maintainer.md`, IDEAS I24 and I25, read after the twelve points were written). Their verdict: they quit Arbor and went back to macOS's own tiling, which for how they work today is better. The twelve points stand. What the handoff adds or sets right:
  - **The order.** Faults against the seven rules still come first. What the first use found counts among them at the same weight (I25 a). Then I20, I22, I23, before any further parity item.
  - **What "better" means.** Not the parity score, which rises with every feature and never falls for clutter: that is how Arbor came to some fifty keys. **The bar is macOS alone.** The numbers a change is judged by: unasked actions, nought; journeys lost against macOS's own tiling, none; the budget kept; no clash with a common app; no complaint unanswered. Parity stays as a list.
  - **The instruments come first** (board 0.A to 0.F), so that a night can be judged without a person: the budget as one test; unasked actions and the same journeys without Arbor; then the newcomer's eval in its slower form through Git. A key for a model at the runner's end costs something each month: the maintainer's to grant, and nobody asks.
  - **The ten core shortcuts are nine**, as in 1. The tenth place stays free until use asks for it.
  - **I19 was decided before it was met** (Q22, board 1.22: Arbor stands aside) and had not landed. A decision that is not in the build a person tries is worth nothing to them: what is decided against a rule goes to the head of the line.
  - **⌃⌥↩ fills the screen with any window**, a floating one too, which stays floating (board 0.16): "make one window fill the screen and keep it there" is a newcomer's third task, and their window was never docked.
  - **Every complaint a test** (I25 e): `collab/scenarios/wanted/`.
- **What it does not overturn:** the seven rules; what Arbor is for (arrange once, fill the screen with one, one key brings the arrangement back); undo, pause and Quit as the ways out; everything about giving sizes back.

## Decided by the executor

One line per product decision made while building, newest last.

- (app shell review, 2026-10-14) `arbor://` URLs run only camera and arrangement commands unless `allowAllURLCommands` (default false) is on; commands are refused unless listed. `arrange` is allowed although it rebuilds the tree, because TrellisWM publishes it (T71); `float-all` is not; `save-view` is allowed as a camera command. Queries are refused: a URL has nowhere to send the answer. A refusal beeps and is logged.
- (app shell review, 2026-10-14) `exclude` and `include` take only letters, digits, `.`, `-` and `_` (255 bytes at most). Apps with other bundle ids can be excluded from the menu or with `exclude` while focused.
- Review (keys): `docs/KEYBOARD.md` is where default shortcuts are assigned and reserved. ⌃⌥ looks, ⌃⌥⌘ moves, ⇧ is the counterpart, ⌃⌥ + letter is a verb's initial; no ⌃⌥⌘ + letter, no four-modifier chords.
- Review (words): `docs/GLOSSARY.md` is where user-visible words are fixed. Windows outside the view are "out of view"; "tile" is a noun only; users see "tab group", "gap", "shortcut", "frame", "macOS Spaces".
- F40: `pause` brings every window into view instead of freezing things as they are, because nothing reacts to ⌘Tab during a pause and a window left out of view would be lost. For a shared screen, where that is unwelcome, see F58.
- F40: during a pause only shortcuts bound to `toggle-pause` or `resume` stay registered, and both pointer taps are removed. A pause does not survive a relaunch. `pause <minutes>` takes up to 1440.
- F40: `state` during a pause lists no windows and no frames (Arbor is not looking) and still shows the arrangement that will come back.
- Review: `zoom` without a window, when that window is already framed, un-frames: back to the previous view if that was wider, otherwise one level out. `zoom <id>` stays idempotent for scripts.
- Review, F32: `float-all` covers every display; `float-all here` and `arrange all` are the other scopes; `arrange` stays with the display under the pointer.
- Review: `home` (⌃⌥0) shows everything on every display; `show-all` (⌃⌥Space) keeps acting on the display under the pointer.
- Review: a shortcut whose command fails beeps, as a menu item already did. Gestures do not. F43 would replace the beep with the reply on screen.
- Review: replies name spaces by app ("Mail and Notes (n5)", "the Desktop", "everything", "5 windows (n7)" from four parts up), never by window title, because every reply is logged.
- Review: a docked window that cannot grow to its slot is centred in it, and is not asked again while its slot and its frame stay as they were; `relayout` asks every window again.
- Review: an app becoming active reports its **focused** window, not its main window, so a click on the wallpaper (which activates Finder) does not bring a Finder window into view.
- (controller review, 2026-10-14) The camera follows a focus change only when the window really has the keyboard; an app that changes its focused window in the background does not move it. Not rate-limited: an app that takes the keyboard is shown.
- (controller review, 2026-10-14) Camera commands keep acting on the display under the pointer, also from the keyboard, as macOS's ⌃← and ⌃→ do; a command that fails says so when there are several displays.
- (controller review, 2026-10-14) A floating window counts as on the Desktop, and is kept on it as docked windows come and go, if it sticks out by no more than two gaps or is still where Arbor put it. One the user put further over docked windows is left alone until the Desktop next comes into view.
- (controller review, 2026-10-14) A floating window pushed aside or shrunk goes back to the frame the user gave it as soon as the Desktop has room again.
- (controller review, 2026-10-14) Floating windows are parked when the Desktop is squeezed into a tile, and switching to one opens the Desktop.
- (controller review, 2026-10-14) Learned minimum sizes do not expire. Seeing the window smaller lowers them, and `relayout` forgets them all.
- (controller review, 2026-10-14) Minimizing or hiding the framed window keeps the camera on it: its surroundings show meanwhile, and it comes back framed.
- (controller review, 2026-10-14) A docked window stepped out of its group with the keyboard takes half of its new neighbour's slot, or an even share of the row or column when half would be too small to use.
- (controller review, 2026-10-14) History and saved views that frame one window follow it when it is docked somewhere else.
- (controller review, 2026-10-14) A window in macOS's own full screen keeps its slot, takes no space and is not touched.
- (controller review, 2026-10-14) `exclude` and `include` of an app already in that state succeed and say that nothing changed.
- F37: the focus ring is on by default, 3 points, in the accent colour. Only windows Arbor arranges get one: with the keyboard in a sheet, a dialog, a panel or an excluded app there is no ring at all, rather than one on the window behind.
- F37: `focusRingRadius` defaults to 0, meaning "what this macOS gives windows" (17 on macOS 26, measured on a screenshot; 10 before, not measured), not to 16: settings.json is written out with its defaults, so a stored number could not be told from a choice and would be wrong on another system.
- F37: the line is outside the window as far as the gap has room and inside for the rest; on a side where the window reaches the edge of the usable screen it moves inside, so a floating window that fills the screen still shows its ring.
- F37: a click moves the ring when the button goes down, not when it comes up. The ring hides only once something moves or is resized with the button down.
- F37: the ring flashes when it goes to another window, or comes back after being gone, within 0.5 s of any command or camera move (also a clicked tile, and ⌘Tab to a window out of view). Clicks and the end of a drag do not flash. The flash is a band three times the line's width, outwards only, fading in 0.25 s. "Reduce motion" switches the flash off (the coordinator's decision); the ring itself stays.
- F37: the ring's panel is at the normal window level directly above the focused window, not at the floating level, so floating panels, picture-in-picture videos, popovers and F02's stage cover it like the window itself.
- R6, 2026-10-14: `shipped` in `parity.json` means a TrellisWM user who tried the thing the way the reference describes it would agree
  that Arbor does it; a claim that fails on real windows is `partial` (T07, T36, T63). Written into `collab/README.md`.
- R6: in arbor-sim a command that fails makes the run fail unless `expect fail` follows, and a scenario without `expect` fails.
- R6: F34 does not close other apps' windows when one tab's red button is pressed, whatever `windowButtonsActOnGroup` suggests: T15 is inferred.
- F33 (T31, Q2): the switch for what `in` steps toward is not in F33's pull request after all. F56 builds `stepInToward` (`"auto"`, `"pointer"`, `"focus"`) whole, with the display a shortcut acts on, so F33 adds nothing of its own for Q2.
- F33 (T11): the setting is `previewDrags` (F09's key and TrellisWM's), not the spec's `dropPreview`.
- F33 (T05): the setting is `gapHandles` with three values (the product owner's decision): `"shown"`, `"hidden"` (the gaps work, nothing is drawn) and `"off"` (nothing to hover, click or drag). It replaces the spec's boolean `seamHandles` and F15's `showGapHandles`. Any other text counts as `"shown"`, is logged when the file is read, and is left in the file as written.
- F33 (T06): with `shareRemovedSpace` off "nothing else moves" holds to the point: the neighbour also gets the freed gap. For that the layout takes each weight to cover a slot and the gap after it, so with unequal weights frames differ by a few points between the two settings, and flipping the switch nudges windows once. As a bonus, docking beside a window then moves nobody else either.
- F33 (T06): the neighbour is the one before in the group (after, for the first), whether or not it is there: space left to a minimized neighbour shows on whoever stands in for it and belongs to the minimized window when it returns. Moving a docked window somewhere else counts as leaving. A tab leaving its group frees nothing.
- F33 (T52): the setting is `shrinkOutOfView`, not the spec's `parkSmall`: the glossary keeps "park" out of anything a user reads, settings keys included.
- F33 (T52): "small" is 320 × 200 at most, each side on its own; a smaller window is not enlarged. An app that refuses keeps the size it took and is placed again so that still only one point shows. Nothing learned in the corner counts as a minimum size.
- F33 (T52): at launch, a window the saved arrangement knows that has less than 5 % of itself on any display counts as parked and gets its slot or floating frame back. One nobody remembers is rescued as before, and a side of it that measures exactly 320 (width) or 200 (height) becomes 800 or 600.
- (controller review, 2026-10-14, after the product owner's replay) Only a move of the view takes a floating window out of view or gathers windows onto a Desktop that was not showing. A change of the arrangement never does. Replaces "floating windows are parked when the Desktop is squeezed into a tile": they stay where they are, over the docked windows, until the view moves.
- (controller review, 2026-10-14) A `dock` command (keyboard walk, edge, beside) keeps a Desktop that is in view and has windows on it at a usable size, taking what it lacks from the neighbour with most to give, unless that would make another tile.
- (controller review, 2026-10-14) "Put over docked windows on purpose" means moved or resized by the user. A new window still where its app opened it makes way like any other.
- (controller review, 2026-10-14) Floating windows may reach the edge of the screen on the sides where nothing is docked beside the Desktop; the outer gap is for docked windows.
- (controller review, 2026-10-14) Commands that act on a window are refused while it is full screen ("w412 is full screen", F30).
- (controller review, 2026-10-14) When the window with the keyboard is closed, minimized or hidden and macOS hands the keyboard to a window out of view, the view stays and a window in view (under the pointer, else top-left) gets the keyboard. Only if nothing is in view does the view follow. A focus change within 0.3 s of the loss counts as macOS's, but after a close or a minimize only if it goes to a window of the same app (macOS picks no other): another app's window is the user's own ⌘Tab or Dock click and is followed at once. Later changes are the user's too. Replaces the earlier "the window that inherits the keyboard is shown".
- (controller review, 2026-10-14) An app that takes the keyboard without any such loss (it activates itself) is still shown; holding the view against that is F58.
- (controller review, 2026-10-14) Parking places are judged by where macOS really stops a window (32 points above the bottom of the visible frame), over all displays on every side; the place that shows least wins.
- (F57, 2026-10-14) A new window that opens while one window is framed is a guest of that view by default (`newWindowsWhileFramed: "guest"`). Moving it does not end the visit, unlike the spec: a nudge would bring the lost-window problem back. Docking it, closing its host, `arrange` and `float-all` do.
- (F57, 2026-10-14) With the Desktop in view a guest sits on the Desktop like any floating window; it returns to its own frame when its host is framed again. Switching to a guest out of view frames its host, not the Desktop.
- (Accessibility review and F17, 2026-10-14) A native macOS tab group (Finder, Terminal, Xcode) is one window to Arbor: the tab in front has the group's slot or floating frame, and the other tabs are not arranged at all. A tab dragged out is a new window.
- (Accessibility review and F17, 2026-10-14) A window that leaves its app's list alive while another window of the same app joins it in the same place (top left corner and width to the point, height within 40 points: Terminal grows by its tab bar) counts as a tab change; the tab bar is not asked for.
- (Accessibility review and F17, 2026-10-14) A drag whose mouse-up nobody saw is called off: a docked window goes back to its slot, a floating one stays where it is. It is not dropped where the pointer happens to be.
- (Accessibility review and F17, 2026-10-14) After three put-backs in three seconds a window is left where its app wants it until the display is next laid out for another reason (F17 H2's "next structural change").
- (Accessibility review and F17, 2026-10-14) Only the focused window of the frontmost app is reported as focused; it is reported when the app comes forward and when it changes.
- (Accessibility review and F17, 2026-10-14) F17 H1 reads the pressed window's frame from the window server, not through Accessibility: it is about 0.1 s ahead during a drag and cannot hang.
- (Accessibility review and F17, 2026-10-14) Accessibility calls wait one second; the first that times out is the last made to that app until it answers again.
- (Accessibility review and F17, 2026-10-14) Display changes are reported 1.5 s after the last notification when displays, their frames or the primary display changed, 0.3 s when only the Dock or menu bar did, and not at all when nothing did or everything is back as it was.
- (Accessibility review and F17, 2026-10-14) F47 is narrower than its spec: only `AXDialog` and `AXSystemDialog`, and only when one of the app's own windows is out of view. Arbor repairs what it caused and nothing else.
- (app shell review, 2026-10-14) A saved slot whose window does not exist at launch waits 180 s for a window of the same app and title (also one renamed to that title); a slot whose window number still exists waits as long as it does. A window with another title takes the slot if its app has exactly one slot waiting and exactly one window that is not docked (titles such as Mail's rarely survive a reboot); with more of either, only exact titles count. `arrange` and `float-all` give up the waiting slots of their display. Not a setting.
- (app shell review, 2026-10-14) Arbor restarts itself after an unclean end: twice within 120 s, the third time it only rescues the windows, sets state.json aside and quits. Force Quit therefore brings Arbor back; Quit does not. `ARBOR_NO_WATCHDOG=1` turns it off.
- (app shell review, 2026-10-14) A second Arbor with the same home folder refuses to start (alert, stderr, log, exit code 75). Different home folders may run side by side.
- (app shell review, 2026-10-14) Window titles in state.json stay in clear text; the protection is 0600 files in a 0700 folder (reasons in ARCHITECTURE.md, "Privacy").
- (app shell review, 2026-10-14) A state.json from a newer version, or unreadable, is set aside as state.unreadable.json and Arbor starts fresh. A workspace whose tree is broken is dropped whole; its windows float.
