Whatever else ends it leaves the view as it is, and ⌃⌥[ goes back: putting the view back first and laying out after moved every window twice (rule 3).
- Product owner, 2026-10-15 (F61, rule 4): when the app of the window being typed in makes that window wider than its place and refuses less, so that it and its neighbour no longer fit, the neighbour becomes the tile and the window with the keyboard stays. The window with the keyboard is never the tile. Only it can cost a neighbour its place: a background app that grows never does, and for every other window "nothing goes back because an app has moved its own window" stands. Nothing is put back by itself afterwards: the next layout the person causes does that.
- Product owner, 2026-10-15 (F61, the window with the keyboard, further): for it alone there are three steps: what can be spared; what Arbor lent to the others in view goes back; and if that is not enough it takes what it needs from neighbours in view, at the cost of their place, written down as a loan, so that a gap the person set is not forgotten and is back to the point when the loan is no longer needed. The frames are a function of the person's gaps, of who is there and of who has the keyboard, never of the road (Q20). It is worked out only at a layout the person causes: a bare change of the keyboard between two windows in view moves nothing. Two rests stand: a window that fits nowhere, for which nobody pays; and a place the person's own hand made short (T20). In neither does the keyboard stay in the tile: it goes to a neighbour in view.
- Product owner, 2026-10-15 (rule 3; how small apps go): only asking tells how small a window goes, so two strangers that cannot both fit cost one of them a width that goes there and back. Accepted for the first fix, not for the gate. The cure: Arbor remembers each app's smallest size by bundle id across runs, to order and foresee, never as the truth; and it ships with a table of the smallest sizes of common apps, measured on hosted Macs, so that a person's first minute meets no stranger among the stock apps. Bundle id, width, height and the version of macOS: no titles, no paths.

- Q27, 2026-10-15 (the file): the mark is `"format": 2`, a key like any other in the file and no setting. The upgrade happens at a launch only, never at a reload: nobody's file changes under their hands. A copy of the file as it was stays beside it (`settings.before-format-2.json`), since it is somebody's file and the change cannot be taken back. A file Arbor cannot read, copy or write is left as it is and looked at again at the next launch; a value it could not use stays for whoever wrote it. Numbers are compared by value, lists and objects without their white space. The log names the keys taken out, never a value. `set` to the very default takes the key out, as `reset` does. An emptied list or object (`bindings`, `rules`, the lists of apps) may stay in the file as `[]` or `{}`: their default is empty for good. The frozen table has two defaults for two keys: `frameRectangleModifier` (⇧⌘ until 2026-10-14, which cures K1787) and `guardDesktopClicks` (on until then). Whoever chose one of those two on purpose cannot be told from a file of those days and loses the choice at the upgrade: the notes name both. The first copy stays: a later file without the mark is upgraded too, and does not replace it. **Known and left:** the upgrade happens where the file is first read, which is before a second Arbor finds out that it is the second and leaves; and in a marked file a value written by hand that equals the default stays when `reset` finds nothing to change.

The second round is judged against the first, the third against the fourth; the fourth against the first shows what Arbor left in the path of input and how far the Mac itself drifted, and a kind for which the Mac drifted by more than the limit says nothing in that journey. - Product owner, 2026-10-15 (board 1.46; the bare tapper): macOS drops all input behind any tap whose owner has lost the right, says nothing, times nothing out, for ever. So the marks are absolute (300 and 550 ms), not "over the floor": there is none. The verdict needs two roads: the one on which the right goes first, where the marks are held, and the switch itself, clicked by the pointer, which says what a person meets. And "for ever" changes the worst case: one stuck thread of Arbor's at the wrong moment would leave a Mac deaf, with no key to quit Arbor by. Asked of the fixer, as questions with a price, not yet ruled: a last net outside that thread (the watchdog kills an Arbor whose clock has stopped beating while taps are in; input back within 3 s), and fewer taps for less time (IDEAS I26).
- Product owner, 2026-10-15 (board 1.32, how small apps go; the design after two adversarial reads): what Arbor foresees is kept apart from what windows have answered. No plan stands on foresight unasked where asking is free: a window whose slot is exactly what is foreseen is asked, at its planned place, for a step less, so that right foresight is one seen change and an answer; whoever is out of view is asked there. Only docked windows teach or disprove; a disproved side becomes unknown. Where asking is not free (a lender in view with a slot larger than its entry) Arbor believes the paper: a wrong entry costs the neighbour its place until the lender next goes out of view, which a click on the tile brings about, a hand on the gap, `relayout`, a smaller docked window of that app, or thirty days. Not a there-and-back by the calendar: that is a jolt nobody asked for, by a rule nobody could make sense of from what they see. From a home that has met the apps, walks with a window moved twice fall from 5,333 to 1,157 in 10,000; from an empty home hardly at all: so the shipped table is the cure for a first day.
- Product owner, 2026-10-15 (F25; a key in an entry of `displays` that Arbor does not read): no problem, and not counted as one at a reload: unknown keys are ignored. `arborctl displays` names it, the log says only how many. To come, small: `doctor` lists keys that are not read, at the top level and in `displays` alike, as a note under its verdict.
- Product owner, 2026-10-15 (board 1.46; a mark that no design can meet is no mark): "no up lost whose down arrived" cannot be absolute with any hole above zero: a release can fall into it, and Arbor cannot post one without the permission. It becomes **nothing stays stuck**: after the hole the next press and release arrive whole, and the window server's and the app's idea of what is down is right again after that one act, for the button, a key and a modifier; no drag goes on with nobody holding, no key repeats. Bearable because the one time a person takes the permission away they do it with a click on the switch, whose release has arrived before anything is withdrawn; a button held at that instant takes a script or an administrator. The beat is 0.15 s: the worst case without an answer is two and a half beats, and a plain beat of the probe's stream shows as 120 to 138 ms with nobody running. macOS's silence takes the taps out and counts for nothing else: it is no second opinion that the permission is gone.
- Product owner, 2026-10-15 (board 1.34, what is found counts as left; three riders): where Arbor is the reason that a window lies mostly out of view (another window of its app sits where Arbor puts windows out of view), Arbor fetches it, once, whether it opened or was found at a start; not while that sibling is in the Dock or hidden by its person. A floating window that went out with the view is given back by Quit, pause and Float All exactly where its person left it, however little shows: a Quit that tidies moves what it never took. What stays gets the note a hand leaves, so that no later ⌃⌥0, dock or closing pulls it in. And displays that changed while Arbor was off: the windows macOS moved count as left. Arbor moves nothing at a start that it did not itself put away.
- Product owner, 2026-10-15 (the gate's rounds of input; read in the pull request): an event that never arrives must be judged. A round says what it posted, by kind; fewer arrivals than were posted is a `FAIL` with Arbor and the instrument's own without; a sabotage drops one. It is what macOS does behind a tap gone wrong: dropped, never late. And a kind counts as judged in a journey only if both pairs have their ten events: the round before the last Quit, with an arrangement at work, is the more telling of the two.
- Product owner, 2026-10-15 (board 1.46; which question turns): only a trial tap. On the road where the right goes first it is refused 0 to 33 ms after the first dropped event, at about a third of a millisecond a call, and in 10,169 calls it never took longer than 81 ms to answer. The flag, "is my tap still enabled?" and both preflights never turn there, and the callback is told nothing. So the clock's question is the trial tap: "Arbor goes by the tap, not by the flag", confirmed from the other side. At that price a beat of 50 ms is two thirds of a per cent of a core: too much for all day, nothing if taps are in only while Arbor's modifiers are down (IDEAS I26).
- Product owner, 2026-10-15 (Q16's waiting place; four rules a person can meet): room is kept only while everything shows: a view on a part of the arrangement is filled by who is there, and back at everything the hole is where it was. Never at a window's cost: a hole that would make a tile of a window that could show gets no room, and that display's holes are then given up for that morning: "a start takes nothing out of view" outranks "a late window finds its place". When the three minutes are up the hole closes with the next layout that moves something anyway, or at `relayout`, not when an unrelated floating window opens or closes: docked windows do not jump for what is not about them. Until then the overdue hole still takes its window: what a person sees is the promise, not the clock; so the guide says "for at least three minutes". `undo` brings no hole back: it gives back what a person changed, and a hole is Arbor's waiting.

- Product owner, 2026-10-15 (there and back is nothing; three edges): the keyboard wins: a window that got room while another was away and is being typed in stays in view when the other comes back by itself; the gaps go on from where they are, and that is the arrangement from then on. "There and back is nothing" is a promise about what nobody touched. What Arbor learns meanwhile counts as a change: windows whose smallest sizes became known get room in one move instead of going back into tiles; the same for a display that changed size. And a window that was in the Dock when the person last moved a gap belongs, once back, to the set whose gaps come back. The root under all of it is board 1.51.

What counts is the road: for each of x, y, width and height, what the window travelled in that step less the straight distance from where it stood to where it ended up. Over 6 points is a jolt, whoever answered what. So 900 → 557 in place and then moved at 557 is none; 900 → 574 → 710 is one. The walks' baseline is taken again once their net has learnt it: what was never a jolt is not booked as a cure.

- Product owner, 2026-10-15 (what turns `pointerGestures` to true): the sentence to be made true is "Arbor stands in the path of your input only while you hold its keys" (board 1.53). At the shipped defaults no tap is on with no key held: ⇧ alone is not one of Arbor's keys; the pinch on the bare Desktop without ⌥ and ⇧-drag on the Desktop become settings, off: no finger has ever tried either on a real trackpad, and safety outranks a nicety nobody has felt. What needs a tap on all day says so where it is switched on. The last net is in the process, not a kill from outside: with the clock on the thread that answers the taps, a thread that stands brings macOS's own timeout, and "for ever" needs taps that answer while nobody asks; so the asking thread notes each beat and, with taps in and none for a second, invalidates the ports itself. The key turns when 1.46 is within its marks on a Mac, 1.53 and that net have landed, and the mended stream says that nothing stays stuck.
